- LAMBADA: +5.15pp, p<10⁻⁶
- ARC-Easy: +1.15pp, p=6×10⁻⁵
- HellaSwag: -0.06pp, p=0.374 (n.s.)
- PIQA: -0.49pp, p=0.006

修改要点：
1. §4.1 中描述 routing 各 benchmark 表现的段落，把 PIQA 从"no benefit"/"持平"类表述改为明确说 significantly negative（-0.49pp, p=0.006）。保持简洁，一句话即可。

3. 不要改 LAMBADA 和 ARC-Easy 的数字（论文现有数字 +5.3pp/+1.0pp 来自 unpaired，paired 结果一致方向，保持现有即可）。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QnMr9rBpzWi1hfJAuoCJ1K`</summary>

```text
39	\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74; Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation \citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.
40	
41	\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, rescue-threshold routing achieves 56.2\% accuracy versus 50.9\% for majority vote ($+5.3$\,pp, SD $= 0.8$\,pp across 10 splits), extracting 36\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\%). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\,pp, SD $= 0.9$\,pp); on HellaSwag and PIQA, routing provides no benefit. We note that confidence-weighted majority vote and other ensemble strategies may provide comparable or superior gains; systematic comparison is left to future work. Using 10-repeat 50/50 cross-validated splits, routing works when multiplicity is high ($\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but fails when multiplicity is low ($\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).
42	
43	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\alpha$ despite different formats. Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Appendix, Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) share a reasoning-task floor, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce higher values.
44	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PANSYFkRwCrKoUoZVJGBX2`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Per-example multiplicity in hybrid recurrent models varies by task ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$), with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$) even as aggregate variance decreases on LAMBADA (non-significant; Table~\ref{tab:convergence}) (dependent on GDN inclusion; \S\ref{ssec:robustness}); five-architecture validation confirms this across four benchmarks (all $J \leq 0.14$, $p < .01$; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}). Pure-model confidence partially predicts affected examples (AUC 0.70--0.74; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
4	
5	At the 340M--1.3B scales studied here, two implications for practitioners follow from the ${\sim}90\%$ non-overlap: (1) $\alpha > 0.2$ warrants per-example audit before architecture substitution, as multiplicity at this level means $>$20\% of examples change outcome; (2) low-Jaccard pairs maximize ensemble complementarity---majority vote fails despite 15--30\% disagreement, but oracle accuracy reveals $+7$--$14$\,pp of untapped gain. Preliminary results suggest rescue-threshold routing may partially exploit this complementarity on high-$\alpha$ tasks ($+5.3$\,pp on LAMBADA; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}), but further validation across tasks and scales is needed.
6	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nPer-example multiplicity in hybrid recurrent models varies by task ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$), with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$) even as aggregate variance decreases on LAMBADA (non-significant; Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}) (dependent on GDN inclusion; \\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}); five-architecture validation confirms this across four benchmarks (all $J \\leq 0.14$, $p < .01$; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}).           " ",
          " \\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74; Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation \\citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, rescue-threshold routing achieves 56.2\\% accuracy versus 50.9\\% for majority vote ($+5.3$\\,pp, SD $= 0.8$\\,pp across 10 splits), extracting 36\\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\\%). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\\,pp, SD $= 0.9$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no significant benefit ($-0.06$\\,pp, $p = .374$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.49$\\,pp, $p = .006$). We note that confidence-weighted majority vote and other ensemble strategies may provide comparable or superior gains; systematic comparison is left to future work. Using 10-repeat 50/50 cross-validated splits, routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but fails when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).",
          "+\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\\,pp, SD $= 0.9$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no significant benefit ($-0.06$\\,pp, $p = .374$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.49$\\,pp, $p = .006$). We note that confidence-weighted majority vote and other ensemble strategies may provide comparable or superior gains; systematic comparison is left to future work. Using 10-repeat 50/50 cross-validated splits, routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).",

          " \\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\\alpha$ despite different formats. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) share a reasoning-task floor, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce higher values.",

```text
64	\subsection{Aggregate Trends and Per-Example Independence}\label{ssec:decoupling}
65	
66	The per-example findings above raise a natural question: when aggregate metrics converge, does this reflect agreement on the same examples? Table~\ref{tab:convergence} provides context. Only LAMBADA shows a monotonically decreasing variance pattern ($\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN, whose h3-1 uses 16 rather than 32 attention heads: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). This is a suggestive but underpowered secondary observation ($n = 5$; the test has negligible power for $|\rho| < 1.0$). Given the limited statistical power, non-significant correlations on other benchmarks should not be interpreted as absence of association. LAMBADA relies purely on in-context retrieval without external knowledge or multi-step reasoning, making it the task most directly aligned with attention's role in context access---a plausible explanation for why convergence is strongest here.
67	
68	\begin{figure}[t]
69	\centering
70	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}
71	\caption{Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}
72	\label{fig:task_specificity}
73	\end{figure}
74	
75	\begin{figure*}[t]
76	\centering
77	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf}
78	\caption{Pairwise Jaccard heatmap across five architectures (340M, h3-1), where helped (hurt) examples are those correct only with (without) attention. Upper triangle: $J_{\text{helped}}$; lower triangle: $J_{\text{hurt}}$. All values $\leq 0.14$, all permutation tests $p \leq .002$. HellaSwag and PIQA results in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}.}
79	\label{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}
80	\end{figure*}
81	
82	Even on LAMBADA, the answer is no. Aggregate variance decreases while per-example overlap remains constant as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
83	
84	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases from 0.024 (pure) to 0.005 (h3-1) (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; non-significant), while pairwise Jaccard saturates (i.e., remains at a low plateau with no further increase) at the first attention level ($\bar{J} \approx 0.095$)---indicating that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
85	
86	This saturation generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_saturation}): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11.
87	
88	\begin{figure}[t]
89	\centering
90	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_jaccard_saturation.pdf}
91	\caption{Cross-benchmark Jaccard saturation. Mean pairwise $\bar{J}$ (averaged over helped and hurt sets) remains flat across attention ratios on all four benchmarks (all $\rho \leq 0.80$, $p \geq .333$), confirming that per-example divergence saturates immediately regardless of task type.}
92	\label{fig:jaccard_saturation}
93	\end{figure}
94	
95	Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm non-overlapping $\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}). Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_hierarchy} shows a three-tier Jaccard hierarchy: independence baseline (${\sim}0.05$), cross-architecture (${\sim}0.10$, $2\times$ baseline), and within-architecture (${\sim}0.33$, $3\times$ cross-architecture; Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}). Pythia 70M seed calibration produces a comparable within-seed level ($J = 0.27$--$0.39$; Appendix~\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-driven divergence, though matched multi-seed experiments would be needed for definitive evidence.
96	
97	\begin{figure}[t]
98	\centering
99	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_jaccard_hierarchy.pdf}
100	\caption{Jaccard hierarchy on LAMBADA (1.3B). Cross-architecture overlap ($\bar{J} \approx 0.09$) is $2\times$ the independence baseline but only one-third of within-architecture overlap ($\bar{J} \approx 0.33$). Pythia 70M within-seed Jaccard (different scale) falls at a comparable level. Error bars show the helped--hurt range.}
101	\label{fig:jaccard_hierarchy}
102	\end{figure}
103	
104	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing variance reduction despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no variance reduction with the lowest multiplicity.
105	
106	Cross-scale analysis confirms scale-robustness: pairwise Jaccard is stable ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at both scales), though $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp). At 340M, aggregate variance reduction is absent ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$), suggesting that the \emph{pattern} of multiplicity reflects structural differences while absolute magnitude is modulated by capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}). Future work with additional architectures or attention ratios could employ mixed-effects models to improve statistical power beyond the $n = 5$ rank-based tests used here.
107	
108	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
109	
110	Removing GDN weakens the LAMBADA variance reduction pattern ($\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$) and reverses MMLU's ($\rho = +0.6$), both driven by GDN's 16-head configuration (vs.\ 32 in others; Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the independence pattern ($\sigma \leq 0.008$, $J = 0.086$--$0.108$). The aggregate variance pattern is therefore dependent on GDN inclusion; the decoupling result is not. Five-architecture validation at 340M (GLA, DeltaNet added) confirms $J \leq 0.14$ across all ten pairs and four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Figure~\ref{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}). Three-architecture results are stable across both 340M and 1.3B scales (Jaccard $\approx$ 0.08--0.10), providing indirect support for scale robustness. Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}.
111	
```

          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74; Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation \\citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, rescue-threshold routing achieves 56.2\\% accuracy versus 50.9\\% for majority vote ($+5.3$\\,pp, SD $= 0.8$\\,pp across 10 splits), extracting 36\\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\\%). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\\,pp, SD $= 0.9$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no significant benefit ($-0.06$\\,pp, $p = .374$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.49$\\,pp, $p = .006$). We note that confidence-weighted majority vote and other ensemble strategies may provide comparable or superior gains; systematic comparison is left to future work. Using 10-repeat 50/50 cross-validated splits, routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).",
          "+\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, rescue-threshold routing achieves 56.2\\% accuracy versus 50.9\\% for majority vote ($+5.3$\\,pp, SD $= 0.8$\\,pp across 10 splits), extracting 36\\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\\%). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\\,pp, SD $= 0.9$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no significant benefit ($-0.06$\\,pp, $p = .374$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.49$\\,pp, $p = .006$). Using 10-repeat 50/50 cross-validated splits, routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\\alpha$ despite different formats. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) share a reasoning-task floor, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce higher values.",
          " "
        ]
      }

646	
647	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Scale Comparison}
648	
649	Table~\ref{tab:340m_multi_bench} extends the scale comparison to all four main benchmarks. Two patterns emerge. First, $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M across all benchmarks, with the largest gap on ARC-Easy ($+$8.4pp) and the smallest on LAMBADA ($+$0.6pp). Second, pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J}_h = 0.08$--$0.10$ at both scales), confirming that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---is scale-robust even as its magnitude increases at smaller scale. Notably, the $\alpha$ inflation does not track accuracy loss: HellaSwag has the largest accuracy gap between scales yet the smallest $\Delta\alpha$ ($+$1.9pp), while ARC-Easy has a smaller accuracy gap but the largest $\Delta\alpha$ ($+$8.4pp), arguing against accuracy as the primary driver of scale-dependent multiplicity.
650	
651	\begin{table}[ht]
652	\centering
653	\small

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JiAje83VMKBnkYajtnFTUd`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Per-example multiplicity in hybrid recurrent models varies by task ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$), with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$) even as aggregate variance decreases on LAMBADA (non-significant; Table~\ref{tab:convergence}) (dependent on GDN inclusion; \S\ref{ssec:robustness}); five-architecture validation confirms this across four benchmarks (all $J \leq 0.14$, $p < .01$; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_arch}). Pure-model confidence partially predicts affected examples (AUC 0.70--0.74; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
4	
5	At the 340M--1.3B scales studied here, two implications for practitioners follow from the ${\sim}90\%$ non-overlap: (1) $\alpha > 0.2$ warrants per-example audit before architecture substitution, as multiplicity at this level means $>$20\% of examples change outcome; (2) low-Jaccard pairs maximize ensemble complementarity---majority vote fails despite 15--30\% disagreement, but oracle accuracy reveals $+7$--$14$\,pp of untapped gain. Preliminary results suggest rescue-threshold routing may partially exploit this complementarity on high-$\alpha$ tasks ($+5.3$\,pp on LAMBADA; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}), but routing is counterproductive on low-$\alpha$ tasks ($-0.49$\,pp on PIQA), and further validation across tasks and scales is needed.
6	
```

</details>

    "oldString": "Per-example multiplicity in hybrid recurrent models varies by task ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$), with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$) even as aggregate variance decreases on LAMBADA (non-significant; Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}) (dependent on GDN inclusion; \\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}); five-architecture validation confirms this across four benchmarks (all $J \\leq 0.14$, $p < .01$; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}). Pure-model confidence partially predicts affected examples (AUC 0.70--0.74; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).\n\nAt the 340M--1.3B scales studied here, two implications for practitioners follow from the ${\\sim}90\\%$ non-overlap: (1) $\\alpha > 0.2$ warrants per-example audit before architecture substitution, as multiplicity at this level means $>$20\\% of examples change outcome; (2) low-Jaccard pairs maximize ensemble complementarity---majority vote fails despite 15--30\\% disagreement, but oracle accuracy reveals $+7$--$14$\\,pp of untapped gain. Preliminary results suggest rescue-threshold routing may partially exploit this complementarity on high-$\\alpha$ tasks ($+5.3$\\,pp on LAMBADA; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}), but routing is counterproductive on low-$\\alpha$ tasks ($-0.49$\\,pp on PIQA), and further validation across tasks and scales is needed.",

So the Conclusion still spills about 2 lines onto page 9. I need to compress a bit more. Let me look at what else I can cut from the main text to save ~2-3 more lines.


Actually, the spillover is quite minimal—just 1.5 lines or so. I should tighten the Conclusion section further. The current version mentions per-example multiplicity varying by task with specific alpha values, notes that Jaccard similarity stays uniformly low across architectures, and states that model confidence partially predicts affected examples with an AUC range. I can condense this by removing some of the technical specifics and focusing on the key findings.

For the second part about the 340M–1.3B scale range, the main points are the ~90% non-overlap leading to two implications: first, high alpha values require per-example audits before switching architectures, and second, low-Jaccard pairs offer strong ensemble complementarity with oracle accuracy showing substantial gains beyond majority voting. The rescue-threshold routing shows mixed results—helping on high-alpha tasks but hurting on low-alpha ones—so more validation is needed.

Let me try removing the "confirmed across five architectures" reference since that's already in the appendix, and see if I can streamline the language around the routing results to save a couple more lines.

Rescue-threshold routing partially exploits this on high-$\\alpha$ tasks ($+5.3$\\,pp on LAMBADA) but is counterproductive on low-$\\alpha$ tasks ($-0.49$\\,pp on PIQA); further validation across tasks and scales is needed.",
          "+Per-example multiplicity in hybrid recurrent models varies by task ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$), with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$). Pure-model confidence partially predicts affected examples (AUC 0.70--0.74). At the 340M--1.3B scales studied here, two implications follow: (1) $\\alpha > 0.2$ warrants per-example audit before architecture substitution; (2) low-Jaccard pairs maximize ensemble complementarity ($+7$--$14$\\,pp oracle gain beyond majority vote). Rescue-threshold routing partially exploits this on high-$\\alpha$ tasks ($+5.3$\\,pp on LAMBADA) but is counterproductive on low-$\\alpha$ tasks ($-0.49$\\,pp on PIQA)."
        ]
      }
    ],

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Per-example multiplicity in hybrid recurrent models varies by task ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$), with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$). Pure-model confidence partially predicts affected examples (AUC 0.70--0.74). At the 340M--1.3B scales studied here, two implications follow: (1) $\alpha > 0.2$ warrants per-example audit before architecture substitution; (2) low-Jaccard pairs maximize ensemble complementarity ($+7$--$14$\,pp oracle gain beyond majority vote). Rescue-threshold routing partially exploits this on high-$\alpha$ tasks ($+5.3$\,pp on LAMBADA) but is counterproductive on low-$\alpha$ tasks ($-0.49$\,pp on PIQA).
4	
```

[Director 指令] [Patch] 数据自查发现的 3 个修复。这次不用验证引用。

**1. [必改] Appendix ECE spread 百分比错误**
在 appendix.tex 中找到 "The cross-architecture ECE spread (17%, computed as range/mean)"，将 "17%" 改为 "6%"。实际值为 5.7%（range=0.013, mean=0.227），17% 是计算错误。

**2. [必改] Table 11 caption 描述不准确**
Table 11 caption 中 "Mean pairwise Jaccard (**helped sets**, 3 architecture pairs)" 改为 "Mean pairwise Jaccard (**mean of helped and hurt sets**, 3 architecture pairs)"。表中数据实际是 helped+hurt 平均值，不仅是 helped。

**3. [建议改] Routing 数值统一来源**
§4.1 routing 段当前混用两个来源。统一使用 paired t-test 值（因为已报 paired p-values）：
- LAMBADA: "+5.3\,pp" → "+5.2\,pp"
- ARC-Easy: "+1.0\,pp" → "+1.2\,pp"
- HellaSwag (-0.06pp) 和 PIQA (-0.49pp) 保持不变（已是 paired 值）
- Abstract 中的 "+5.3 pp" 也同步改为 "+5.2 pp"
- 检查 Conclusion 和其他引用这两个数字的地方，一并更新

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{caption}\n\n\\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nPractitioners select among hybrid linear recurrent architectures using aggregate benchmarks, but these scores can mask which specific examples each architecture gets right. Across three controlled designs, adding attention affects substantially different examples per architecture (Jaccard $\\approx$ 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline); validation across five architectures and ten pairwise comparisons confirms this on all four benchmarks (all $p < .01$). Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects and find that this predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) more than attention dose. Aggregate trends do not reach statistical significance ($n = 5$; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$), yet per-example overlap saturates at 4\\% attention and remains flat across all four benchmarks. Majority vote fails to exploit this complementarity, confirming that configurations interchangeable by aggregate metrics carry hidden per-example consequences. Confidence-informed routing extracts 36\\% of oracle complementarity on LAMBADA ($+5.3$\\,pp vs.\\ majority vote), suggesting these differences are partially actionable.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Single training runs.} Each configuration corresponds to a single training run, so we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects. The $3\\times$ within- vs.\\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap and Pythia 70M seed calibration (within-seed $J$ is 2.7--4.3$\\times$ higher; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_calibration}) provide proxy evidence, and the consistency of significant Jaccard across all 40 architecture--benchmark combinations (all $p < .01$) would be unlikely under seed noise alone. With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable $p$ is 0.017, so LAMBADA's Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$ does not reach conventional significance.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage.} We examine three primary architectures (plus two extended) from one model zoo, four main benchmarks, and two scale points. Generalization to Mamba, RWKV, other hybrid families, and generation tasks remains open. Results at larger scales (e.g., 7B+) may differ, particularly as attention mechanisms become more expressive with increased capacity.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All evaluations use pretrained checkpoints without fine-tuning; per-example effects may not persist after task-specific adaptation.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\n\nAll models are from the publicly available model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis code and per-example evaluation outputs will be released upon publication.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

          " \\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74; Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation \\citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, rescue-threshold routing achieves 56.2\\% accuracy versus 50.9\\% for majority vote ($+5.3$\\,pp, SD $= 0.8$\\,pp across 10 splits), extracting 36\\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\\%). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.0$\\,pp, SD $= 0.9$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no significant benefit ($-0.06$\\,pp, $p = .374$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.49$\\,pp, $p = .006$). Using 10-repeat 50/50 cross-validated splits, routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).",
          "+\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.2$\\,pp, SD $= 0.9$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no significant benefit ($-0.06$\\,pp, $p = .374$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.49$\\,pp, $p = .006$). Using 10-repeat 50/50 cross-validated splits, routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\\alpha$ despite different formats. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) share a reasoning-task floor, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce higher values.",

```text
560	\section{Calibration Analysis}\label{app:calibration}
561	
562	Cross-architecture disruption threshold variation (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}) could in principle arise from calibration differences rather than genuine differences in per-example processing. We compute expected calibration error (ECE; \citealt{guo2017calibration}) for each architecture's h3-1 model on LAMBADA using 15 equal-width bins. ECE values are similar: GDN 0.222, RetNet 0.224, HGRN2 0.235. The cross-architecture ECE spread (17\%, computed as range/mean) is less than half the disruption threshold spread (47\%; Table~\ref{tab:threshold_gap}), indicating that calibration differences are insufficient to account for the observed threshold divergence. Rescue thresholds, which show only 1\% cross-architecture spread, are consistent with near-identical calibration in the low-confidence regime.
563	
564	\section{BoolQ Supplementary Analysis}
```

        "oldStart": 559,

        "newStart": 559,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " ",
          " \\section{Calibration Analysis}\\label{app:calibration}",
          " ",
          "-Cross-architecture disruption threshold variation (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) could in principle arise from calibration differences rather than genuine differences in per-example processing. We compute expected calibration error (ECE; \\citealt{guo2017calibration}) for each architecture's h3-1 model on LAMBADA using 15 equal-width bins. ECE values are similar: GDN 0.222, RetNet 0.224, HGRN2 0.235. The cross-architecture ECE spread (17\\%, computed as range/mean) is less than half the disruption threshold spread (47\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:threshold_gap}), indicating that calibration differences are insufficient to account for the observed threshold divergence. Rescue thresholds, which show only 1\\% cross-architecture spread, are consistent with near-identical calibration in the low-confidence regime.",
          "+Cross-architecture disruption threshold variation (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) could in principle arise from calibration differences rather than genuine differences in per-example processing. We compute expected calibration error (ECE; \\citealt{guo2017calibration}) for each architecture's h3-1 model on LAMBADA using 15 equal-width bins. ECE values are similar: GDN 0.222, RetNet 0.224, HGRN2 0.235. The cross-architecture ECE spread (6\\%, computed as range/mean) is less than half the disruption threshold spread (47\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:threshold_gap}), indicating that calibration differences are insufficient to account for the observed threshold divergence. Rescue thresholds, which show only 1\\% cross-architecture spread, are consistent with near-identical calibration in the low-confidence regime.",
          " ",
          " \\section{BoolQ Supplementary Analysis}",
          " \\label{app:boolq}"
        ]

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user is a Director coordinating an EMNLP 2026 ARR submission for "Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures." This session covers 9 sequential Director patch instructions executed as final pre-submission edits. The Paper Agent communicates with Director exclusively via `paper_agent_post_to_director` — system reminders about `main_agent_post_to_director` are false alarms and are always ignored with "No response requested."

   Sequential Director instructions in this session:
   - **R16-W3 report**: Send pending AUC normality patch report to Director
   - **R17 final polish**: ~15 text replacements (convergence→variance reduction, format confound, scale qualifiers, etc.)
   - **R17 citation patch**: Add Ye et al. (2026), confirm Haghighat exists
   - **Final 5 micro-adjustments**: Intro sentence split, Abstract/Related Work/caption rewrites
   - **Appendix P.2 normality update**: Shapiro-Wilk data replacing prior paragraph
   - **AI disclosure**: Appendix Y for ARR compliance
   - **Routing PIQA update**: "no benefit" → "significantly negative" (-0.49pp, p=.006)
   - **URGENT page overflow**: Compress ~12 lines to fit Conclusion on page 8
   - **Conclusion saturation sentence**: Add second core contribution, maintain page fit
   - **Data self-check**: ECE 17%→6%, Table 11 caption fix, routing values to paired t-test

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Predictive Multiplicity** (Marx et al. 2020): Task-dependent α = 0.14–0.30
   - **Jaccard similarity**: Cross-architecture ≤ 0.10 (3-arch), ≤ 0.14 (5-arch)
   - **Paired t-test routing results**: LAMBADA +5.2pp (p<10⁻⁶), ARC-Easy +1.2pp (p=6×10⁻⁵), HellaSwag -0.06pp (p=.374 n.s.), PIQA -0.49pp (p=.006)
   - **Shapiro-Wilk normality**: Disruption W∈[0.955,0.973] mild skew; Rescue W∈[0.899,0.920] moderate right-skew
   - **ECE calibration**: Corrected spread = 6% (range=0.013, mean=0.227)
   - **EMNLP 2026**: 8-page main text limit (strict); Limitations/Ethics/References/Appendix unlimited; Abstract ≤ 200 words
   - **LaTeX figure* placement**: In two-column mode, figure* goes at top of next page after encountered in source

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`sections/experiments.tex`** (most heavily modified)
     - Convergence→variance reduction: 6 replacements in §4.2 and §4.3
     - §4.1 routing: PIQA now "-0.49pp, p=.006" (significantly negative); HellaSwag "-0.06pp, p=.374" (n.s.); routing values unified to paired: +5.2pp LAMBADA, +1.2pp ARC-Easy
     - §4.2 deletions for page compression: removed "Given the limited statistical power..." sentence, "(i.e., remains at a low plateau...)" parenthetical, "Future work with additional architectures..." sentence, "We note that confidence-weighted majority vote..." sentence
     - §4.3: "Three-architecture results..." moved to Appendix; "preserves decoupling" → "preserves the independence pattern"; "aggregate convergence result" → "aggregate variance pattern"
     - Table 1 caption: "Cross-architecture convergence" → "Cross-architecture variance trends"; added "standard deviation" before σ
     - Figure 1 caption: "Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled" → "Aggregate variance and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories"
     - Table 11 caption: "helped sets" → "mean of helped and hurt sets"

   - **`sections/introduction.tex`**

     ```latex
     Per-example multiplicity in hybrid recurrent models varies by task ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$), with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$). Pure-model confidence partially predicts affected examples (AUC 0.70--0.74). Per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level and remains flat, indicating that additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. At the 340M--1.3B scales studied here, two implications follow: (1) $\alpha > 0.2$ warrants per-example audit before architecture substitution; (2) low-Jaccard pairs maximize ensemble complementarity ($+7$--$14$\,pp oracle gain beyond majority vote), though rescue-threshold routing is counterproductive on low-$\alpha$ tasks ($-0.49$\,pp on PIQA).
     ```

        "content": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{caption}\n\n\\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nPractitioners select among hybrid linear recurrent architectures using aggregate benchmarks, but these scores can mask which specific examples each architecture gets right. Across three controlled designs, adding attention affects substantially different examples per architecture (Jaccard $\\approx$ 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline); validation across five architectures and ten pairwise comparisons confirms this on all four benchmarks (all $p < .01$). Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales), we decompose per-example effects and find that this predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) more than attention dose. Aggregate trends do not reach statistical significance ($n = 5$; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$), yet per-example overlap saturates at 4\\% attention and remains flat across all four benchmarks. Majority vote fails to exploit this complementarity, confirming that configurations interchangeable by aggregate metrics carry hidden per-example consequences. Confidence-informed routing extracts 36\\% of oracle complementarity on LAMBADA ($+5.2$\\,pp vs.\\ majority vote), suggesting these differences are partially actionable.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Single training runs.} Each configuration corresponds to a single training run, so we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects. The $3\\times$ within- vs.\\ cross-architecture Jaccard gap and Pythia 70M seed calibration (within-seed $J$ is 2.7--4.3$\\times$ higher; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_calibration}) provide proxy evidence, and the consistency of significant Jaccard across all 40 architecture--benchmark combinations (all $p < .01$) would be unlikely under seed noise alone. With $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable $p$ is 0.017, so LAMBADA's Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$ does not reach conventional significance.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage.} We examine three primary architectures (plus two extended) from one model zoo, four main benchmarks, and two scale points. Generalization to Mamba, RWKV, other hybrid families, and generation tasks remains open. Results at larger scales (e.g., 7B+) may differ, particularly as attention mechanisms become more expressive with increased capacity.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All evaluations use pretrained checkpoints without fine-tuning; per-example effects may not persist after task-specific adaptation.\n\n\\section*{Code and Data Availability}\n\nAll models are from the publicly available model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis code and per-example evaluation outputs will be released upon publication.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
        "numLines": 57,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 57
      }

        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nUsing the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} (\\S\\ref{ssec:model_zoo}), we evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks plus supplementary COPA ($n = 100$) at 1.3B scale unless noted. Results at 340M appear in scale analyses. Recall: h$k$-1 denotes one attention layer per $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 = 25\\% attention.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-architecture variance trends across benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and cross-architecture standard deviation $\\sigma$ across five configurations. $p$: exact permutation test (two-tailed, 120 permutations). $\\sigma$ values reported $\\times 10^{2}$ (accuracy percentage points). Ratio: $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$. \\textbf{Bold}: only benchmark with $\\rho = -1.0$. HellaSwag evaluated using normalized accuracy (acc\\_norm), the standard metric for this benchmark.}\n\\label{tab:convergence}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Category & $\\rho$ & $p$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}}$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$ & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{LAMBADA$^{\\ddagger}$} & \\textbf{Retrieval} & $\\mathbf{-1.0}$ & \\textbf{.017} & \\textbf{2.41} & \\textbf{0.49} & \\textbf{4.87} \\\\\n\\quad\\emph{(excl.\\ GDN)} & & $-0.7$ & .233 & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nBoolQ & Comprehension & $0.2$ & .783 & 1.63 & 2.36 & 0.69 \\\\\nARC-C & Reasoning & $0.6$ & .350 & 0.96 & 1.32 & 0.72 \\\\\nHellaSwag & Reasoning & $0.4$ & .517 & 1.17 & 1.22 & 0.96 \\\\\nPIQA & Reasoning & $-0.5$ & .450 & 0.54 & 0.34 & 1.59 \\\\\nWinoGrande & Reasoning & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.67 & 0.78 & 0.86 \\\\\nARC-E & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 1.00 & 0.13 & 7.70 \\\\\nMMLU & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 0.92 & 0.41 & 2.27 \\\\\nSciQ & Mixed & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.87 & 0.74 & 1.19 \\\\\nXStoryCloze & Mixed & $-0.6$ & .350 & 0.82 & 0.55 & 1.49 \\\\\n\\midrule\nCOPA$^{\\dagger}$ & Reasoning & $-0.67$ & .267 & 2.63 & 0.47 & 5.57 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$Supplementary ($n = 100$); excluded from multiple comparison correction.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^{\\ddagger}$GDN h3-1 uses 16 attention heads; all other configurations use 32 (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}).} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{@{}p{\\textwidth}@{}}{\\footnotesize Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .017 \\times 10 = .170$ (all-10-benchmark correction). \\textbf{Power is limited: only $|\\rho| = 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ at $n = 5$.}}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\\label{ssec:per_example}\n\nDo architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, we verify below that it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). This binarization discards confidence magnitude (a transition from 0.51 to 0.49 is treated identically to one from 0.99 to 0.01), so we supplement it with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}) and verify that low Jaccard persists under continuous thresholds (Appendix~\\ref{app:continuous_jaccard}).\n\nEach architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes. GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which adding attention yields a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90$\\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by $J_{\\max}$, observed overlap reaches only 6--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence margin predictor.} Pure-model confidence on the target token predicts which examples will be affected (AUC 0.70--0.74; Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}): rescue thresholds are architecture-invariant (1\\% spread) but disruption thresholds vary 47\\%, explaining cross-architecture Jaccard divergence (Appendix~\\ref{app:prediction_framework}). Calibration differences account for less than half the threshold spread (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}). The rescue pattern generalizes across three benchmarks ($d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}); disruption is weaker on reasoning tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}). This structured disagreement constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} under architectural variation \\citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence-based routing.} As an illustrative case study, the rescue-threshold asymmetry suggests a practical routing strategy: for each example, select the architecture whose pure-model confidence best predicts successful rescue. On LAMBADA, rescue-threshold routing achieves 56.2\\% accuracy versus 50.9\\% for majority vote ($+5.2$\\,pp, SD $= 0.8$\\,pp across 10 splits), extracting 36\\% of the oracle gap (oracle = 65.5\\%). On ARC-Easy, gains are modest ($+1.2$\\,pp, SD $= 0.9$\\,pp); on HellaSwag, routing provides no significant benefit ($-0.06$\\,pp, $p = .374$); on PIQA, it is significantly negative ($-0.49$\\,pp, $p = .006$). Using 10-repeat 50/50 cross-validated splits, routing works when multiplicity is high ($\\alpha = 0.30$, LAMBADA) and a clear rescue-threshold signal exists, but is counterproductive when multiplicity is low ($\\alpha = 0.14$, PIQA).\n\n\\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way) and PIQA (two-choice) produce nearly identical $\\alpha$ despite different formats. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering (all non-overlapping except PIQA--HellaSwag; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) share a reasoning-task floor, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce higher values.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \\citep{marx2020predictive}. $\\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets, averaged across four hybrid attention ratios and three architecture pairs. $\\rho_\\alpha$: Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and $\\alpha$. Benchmarks ordered by $\\alpha$.}\n\\label{tab:multiplicity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & $N$ & $\\alpha$ & $\\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\\rho_\\alpha$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPIQA & 1838 & .14 & .091 & .094 & $0.0$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10042 & .15 & .095 & .085 & $0.1$ \\\\\nARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .092 & .099 & $-0.2$ \\\\\nLAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .096 & .091 & $-0.6$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\par\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\footnotesize Within-benchmark $\\alpha$ variation $< 0.03$ across ratios. BoolQ, WinoGrande: Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Aggregate Trends and Per-Example Independence}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nThe per-example findings above raise a natural question: when aggregate metrics converge, does this reflect agreement on the same examples? Table~\\ref{tab:convergence} provides context. Only LAMBADA shows a monotonically decreasing variance pattern ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; excluding GDN, whose h3-1 uses 16 rather than 32 attention heads: $\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M; Appendix~\\ref{app:convergence_detail}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}). This is a suggestive but underpowered secondary observation ($n = 5$; the test has negligible power for $|\\rho| < 1.0$). LAMBADA relies purely on in-context retrieval without external knowledge or multi-step reasoning, making it the task most directly aligned with attention's role in context access---a plausible explanation for why convergence is strongest here.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}\n\\caption{Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category. Bootstrap CI is heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures.}\n\\label{fig:task_specificity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/jaccard_heatmap_5arch.pdf}\n\\caption{Pairwise Jaccard heatmap across five architectures (340M, h3-1), where helped (hurt) examples are those correct only with (without) attention. Upper triangle: $J_{\\text{helped}}$; lower triangle: $J_{\\text{hurt}}$. All values $\\leq 0.14$, all permutation tests $p \\leq .002$. HellaSwag and PIQA results in Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}.}\n\\label{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nEven on LAMBADA, the answer is no. Aggregate variance decreases while per-example overlap remains constant as attention ratio increases (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\nCross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases from 0.024 (pure) to 0.005 (h3-1) (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; non-significant), while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level ($\\bar{J} \\approx 0.095$)---indicating that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\\bar{J}$ reaches only 6--7\\% of the achievable range above chance ($J_{\\max} \\approx 0.78$--$0.92$), ruling out ceiling constraints (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).\n\nThis saturation generalizes beyond LAMBADA (Figure~\\ref{fig:jaccard_saturation}): on all four main benchmarks, mean pairwise Jaccard shows no significant trend with attention ratio (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq 0.333$), with values uniformly between 0.08 and 0.11.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_jaccard_saturation.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-benchmark Jaccard saturation. Mean pairwise $\\bar{J}$ (averaged over helped and hurt sets) remains flat across attention ratios on all four benchmarks (all $\\rho \\leq 0.80$, $p \\geq .333$), confirming that per-example divergence saturates immediately regardless of task type.}\n\\label{fig:jaccard_saturation}\n\\end{figure}\n\nBootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm non-overlapping $\\sigma$ between pure and h3-1 but completely overlapping Jaccard across all hybrid ratios (\\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}). Figure~\\ref{fig:jaccard_hierarchy} shows a three-tier Jaccard hierarchy: independence baseline (${\\sim}0.05$), cross-architecture (${\\sim}0.10$, $2\\times$ baseline), and within-architecture (${\\sim}0.33$, $3\\times$ cross-architecture; Appendix~\\ref{app:within_arch_stability}). Pythia 70M seed calibration produces a comparable within-seed level ($J = 0.27$--$0.39$; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_calibration}), consistent with architecture-driven divergence, though matched multi-seed experiments would be needed for definitive evidence.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_jaccard_hierarchy.pdf}\n\\caption{Jaccard hierarchy on LAMBADA (1.3B). Cross-architecture overlap ($\\bar{J} \\approx 0.09$) is $2\\times$ the independence baseline but only one-third of within-architecture overlap ($\\bar{J} \\approx 0.33$). Pythia 70M within-seed Jaccard (different scale) falls at a comparable level. Error bars show the helped--hurt range.}\n\\label{fig:jaccard_hierarchy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAttention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing variance reduction despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\\alpha = 0.15$) show no variance reduction with the lowest multiplicity.\n\nCross-scale analysis confirms scale-robustness: pairwise Jaccard is stable ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at both scales), though $\\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\\Delta \\leq 8$\\,pp). At 340M, aggregate variance reduction is absent ($\\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$), suggesting that the \\emph{pattern} of multiplicity reflects structural differences while absolute magnitude is modulated by capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_per_example}).\n\\subsection{Robustness}\\label{ssec:robustness}\n\nRemoving GDN weakens the LAMBADA variance reduction pattern ($\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$) and reverses MMLU's ($\\rho = +0.6$), both driven by GDN's 16-head configuration (vs.\\ 32 in others; Appendix~\\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the independence pattern ($\\sigma \\leq 0.008$, $J = 0.086$--$0.108$). The aggregate variance pattern is therefore dependent on GDN inclusion; the decoupling result is not. Five-architecture validation at 340M (GLA, DeltaNet added) confirms $J \\leq 0.14$ across all ten pairs and four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Figure~\\ref{fig:jaccard_heatmap_5arch}; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}). Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}.\n",
        "numLines": 110,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 110
      }
    },

        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nPer-example multiplicity in hybrid recurrent models varies by task ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$), with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$). Pure-model confidence partially predicts affected examples (AUC 0.70--0.74). Per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level and remains flat, indicating that additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. At the 340M--1.3B scales studied here, two implications follow: (1) $\\alpha > 0.2$ warrants per-example audit before architecture substitution; (2) low-Jaccard pairs maximize ensemble complementarity ($+7$--$14$\\,pp oracle gain beyond majority vote), though rescue-threshold routing is counterproductive on low-$\\alpha$ tasks ($-0.49$\\,pp on PIQA).\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
    },

  "parentUuid": "09cd74f8-317e-4e6d-99ff-491daafae2a9",

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures---including GDN, RetNet, HGRN2, GLA, and DeltaNet---achieve aggregate benchmark scores within 1--2 points of each other at matched parameter budgets. Yet they can differ on up to 90\\% of the individual examples they affect: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones yields helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: aggregate cross-architecture variance decreases on some benchmarks as attention ratio increases, but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by gaining and losing accuracy on different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Across all ten pairwise comparisons among five architectures, overlap in per-example effects remains below 14\\% (all $p < .01$), meaning the choice of backbone is associated with which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate accuracy, which can mask per-example behavioral diversity. Prior work on predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} and underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification,mccoy2020berts} documents this risk for seed variation within a single architecture, but whether the phenomenon extends to architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models remains open. If architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, the choice of backbone carries hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate variance reduction and per-example overlap follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate variance and per-example overlap follow independent trajectories (helped: correct only with attention; hurt: correct only without). (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$, non-significant) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Cross-architecture pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat. Shaded bands are illustrative ($n = 3$).}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nAggregate variance reduction is task-specific and exploratory: of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA shows monotonic variance reduction ($\\rho = -1.0$, Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .170$; observed at 1.3B, absent at 340M). Yet on this benchmark, per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level while aggregate variance continues to decrease (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}).\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation---a source of multiplicity not previously characterized at the per-example level---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with magnitude determined by task type rather than attention dose (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Extended validation across five architectures confirms the pattern: all ten pairwise Jaccard values remain $\\leq 0.14$ across four benchmarks (all $p < .01$; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_arch}). The primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone \\citep[cf.][]{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, though single training runs prevent full disentanglement from seed effects (Limitations).\n    \\item Per-example Jaccard saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) across all four benchmarks and shows no further increase at higher ratios, while aggregate variance shows a non-significant decreasing pattern on LAMBADA (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_bench_jaccard}). This immediate saturation indicates that minimal attention suffices to establish the full pattern of configuration-specific per-example effects; additional attention narrows aggregate gaps without homogenizing which examples each architecture affects. The aggregate convergence component is dependent on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 27,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 27
      }
    },

    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

243	All normalized effect sizes fall below 0.10, confirming that observed overlap reaches only 6--7\% of the theoretical maximum. The low Jaccard values cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
244	
245	\section{ARC-Easy Per-Example Analysis}\label{app:arceasy}
246	
247	Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_effects} reports per-example effect counts on ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$) at 1.3B scale for each attention ratio. Unlike LAMBADA, ARC-Easy shows no aggregate convergence ($\rho = -0.20$, $p = .783$). HGRN2 is net positive at all ratios; GDN and RetNet are mixed.
